PREFACE.



I HAVE to apologize for a stupid error which runs through
the Part, viz., that the *tenens* is often described as A. B.,
**clericwm," &c. Many of my readers will wonder why the
accusative occurs, but they should remember that the ** inter"
at the head of the fine is understood throughout, and excuse a
wearied calendarer for copying mechanically at times.

The present Part discloses vastly more of nicknames than are
found in Part I. An analysis of those found in both Parts may
amuse my readers, wearied with so much calendar.

The attributes of various birds, more than anything* else,
seem to have given the local wit the idea of flinging a happy
nickname, which stuck to its recipient even on such solemn
occasions as appearing before the King's Court to deal with
landed interests. Take for example Coch,^ Cokerel, and
Chauntecler, obviously meant for noisy and obtrusive persons
now gone to dust six centuries ago. Crowe and Ravine may
have been hits at the local undertaker; Curlu and Befoul at
some seamen. Cucku is obvious, while Pye and Jay were no
doubt village jabberers of byegone centuries. Gooi\e\ for
the local simpleton ; while Sparwe and Notehache once had
their meanings, though now lost. Wudcoch was possibly the
earliest absconding debtor, and Popinjay the local dandy.
Raily Sjpynky Drake^ Fynch, Stynt, Sterlyng, Dove, Puniyny,
Koot, Laverok^ Pertrychy Crane, Snowe, Wrenne, and Wilymot
(Guillemot? J also occur.

Animals do not provide so many. Sogye and Pyg occur, and
Bacon naturally follows; but the latter is a Danish personal

• Of course this may in some instances be *' le Cook.'



11.

namo, so its occurrence is unfair, though, it rounds a period.*
Asaeneye [ass-neigh] may be a joke, while Godlamh, Catte, and
Zeche may refer to living things, though the last may be
Ze Zeche, the medical man. Of other animals we find Lyon,
Otere, Hert, Calf, Sheep, Hare, Fox, Cony, Buc, and Bullok.
Fayrweder may be fair-wether or fair weather. Drepecalf,
Calveaberd, and Kedehmh are strange names : possibly the last is
the record of a joke on some Cockney's error. Of fish we have
Fissh, Pickerel, Codlyng, Sterlyng, Whiting, Heryng, Lyng, and
Haddok; while other animal objects are represented by Bee,
Dodman, and Magot.

Of personal attributes we have Blaoomore, Fairman, Fairchild,
Sandy and Brown, TFhitlok and Fayrher, Rughheved and Shirlok,
Senile, Snoute, Bareffot, Whitfoot, Hugeman, Le Graunt, and Petit,
Little, Potekyn, Stalworthman, and Kylman, While Qwyck,
Lyghtfote, Spryngwelle, Thirlewynde, and Scttrry, are the natural
opposites of Laghalday.

Another group is made up of Oldman, Youngman, Baoheler,
Bycheman, Godeman, Wysman, Skileman, Akerman, Fairman,
Cuteman, Bright, Sharp, Cuter, Rightman, Trewehody, Holyman,
Hatewrong, Beafoy, Le Nolle, Pleasaunt, BLillary, Je%tuor, Haly-
day, and Ryghtup.

Yet another is formed by Le Longetot, Parlehen, Love, Bride,
Homeioyfe, Youngehushand, Bolt, Ledelady, Foulhourn, Leman, Bolt,
Maupas, and Child.

Of French nicknames we have Le Bonclero, Le Salteclere,
Le Dauncere, Le Rimur, Le Merveylleus (a frightful story-teller,
I fear), and Parlehen, Maupas, and Beaureper. Brekeheved and
Querel bring us naturally to the untimely end shadowed forth
by Brekerop, Mouse ivy f, Rynghelle, Shakelok, Wendham, f
Fayrweder, Hophop, Cokhok, Midday, Snoute, no doubt had
meanings once. Golde, Tredegold, Turnepeny, Ferthyng^ Pikhone,
Chaffere, Profete, Le Rychmwn, Spendall, Le Poure, and Ualf knight
form a class of their own; as do Grenehod, Le Hattere, Silk,
Bucskyn, Gildensleve, and Cokerhose.

Food is represented by Milhanbred, Pykehun, Fuddyng^ Cake^
Milky Chese, Blacksalty Spyce, PepeVy and Garlyke.

Of strange local and other names there are several : — Est half
the water j^ In the wylghea (willows), In the fen^ In the htrne, In
the lurnej In the medowey In the mor^ Of the stone, De la Butillerie,
and Del Parlour. While of personal names we see — Ftz le
Prestre, Le Persone^s Neve, F%% a la Persone, Fiz Geffrey du Punt,
Heyner le Abhottes servaunt, John le Smythessone, Richa/rd Halte
Maddyessone, and Richard Jonessone Wattesson,

Of the double s endings, which I fancy are Norwegian, over
a dozen occur in this Part — Maddyessone, Magessone, Mahhessone,
Fmmessoney Bassessone, Saressone, Gotchessone, Jonessone^ Wattessone,
AleynssoUj Alicessony Stevensson, Robynessone, Serryessone, and
Uoddesson. The late genuine ' Fitz * is Laurance Fitz Piers in
16 Henry VI.
